<term>Handbook</term> <note>original: "ENCHIRIDIO". Derived from the Greek *en* (in) and *cheir* (hand), this term refers to a manual or guide designed to be portable and easily consulted. It was a common title for instructional texts in the Renaissance and Early Modern periods.</note>

<summary>This page shows the spine of a leather-bound book, identifying the work as a "Handbook" or "Manual" through gold-stamped lettering.</summary>

<keywords>handbook, manual, Enchiridion, bookbinding, gold-tooling, fleur-de-lis</keywords>

**Physical Description of the Volume**

The edges of the paper have darkened to a deep, almost black hue, a common occurrence in older volumes caused by the accumulation of dust over centuries or the tarnishing of original gold leaf gilding. <term>Fore-edge</term> <note>From the Old English *fore* (front), this is the edge of a book's leaves opposite the spine. In some luxury volumes, this area was painted with "hidden" illustrations that only appeared when the pages were fanned.</note>

<term>Binding</term> <note>The structural covering of a book, typically made of wood or cardboard covered in leather or parchment, meant to protect the internal pages.</note>

# Physical Description of the Volume

The image captures the **fore-edge** <note>The "fore-edge" refers to the three edges of the paper block that are not attached to the spine; specifically, this is the side where the reader opens the book.</note> of a thick, historical book. The edges of the paper have darkened to a deep, almost black hue, a common occurrence in older volumes. This darkening is often caused by the accumulation of dust over centuries or the tarnishing of original gold leaf gilding <note>Gilding involved applying thin layers of gold to the edges to protect the paper and show status.</note>. Fragments of the dark leather binding are visible at the top and bottom of the stack of pages.

Early European Books, Copyright © 2012 ProQuest LLC.
Images reproduced by courtesy of The Wellcome Trust, London.
3725/A

<term>Fore-edge</term> <note>Derived from the Old English *fore* (front), this is the edge of a book's leaves opposite the spine. In some luxury volumes, this area was painted with "hidden" illustrations that only appeared when the pages were fanned out.</note>

<term>Binding</term> <note>The structural covering of a book, typically made of wood or cardboard covered in leather or parchment, designed to protect the internal pages and hold them together.</note>

In L'avocat's dictionary <note>Referring to the *Dictionnaire historique-portatif* by Jean-Baptiste L'Avocat (1709–1765), a ubiquitous biographical and bibliographical reference in the 18th century.</note>,
it is stated that this book is
supposed to be by Pope Leo III
And that the most
esteemed editions are those of 1525
and 1584.

It is this pope who
crowned Charlemagne <note>Pope Leo III crowned Charlemagne as Emperor of the Romans on Christmas Day, 800 AD. This historical connection was often used to lend authority to the "Enchiridion," a book of prayers and charms supposedly gifted by the Pope to the Emperor.</note>

<meta>This page is the formal title page of the "Enchiridion of Pope Leo." While the previous page mentioned Pope Leo I and sermons, this volume is a distinct work—a famous collection of prayers and protective charms traditionally attributed to Pope Leo III and associated with Charlemagne. The handwritten library number 66626 is visible in the top right corner.</meta>

# HANDBOOK
<note>original: "ENCHIRIDION," a Greek-derived term for a concise manual or handbook meant to be kept close at hand.</note>
## OF POPE LEO
<note>Referring to Pope Leo III (reigned 795–816 AD). Legend states he gifted this collection of spiritual protections to Charlemagne after the latter's coronation.</note>
### TO THE MOST SERENE EMPEROR
## CHARLES THE GREAT
<note>Known as Charlemagne, the first Holy Roman Emperor.</note>
### GIVEN AS A PRECIOUS GIFT,

->Most recently purged of all errors.<-
<note>original: "Nuperrimè mendis omnibus purgatum." This was a common claim in 17th-century publishing to suggest a more accurate or corrected text than previous editions.</note>

<image-desc>A circular woodcut emblem featuring the Chi-Rho symbol (a combination of the Greek letters Chi 'X' and Rho 'P', representing the first two letters of "Christ") in the center. The surrounding circular border contains the Latin phrase "IN HOC VINCE," which translates to "In this [sign], conquer."</image-desc>
<note>This motto is historically associated with the vision of Constantine the Great before the Battle of the Milvian Bridge.</note>

### AT MAINZ.
<note>original: "MOGUNTIÆ," the Latin name for Mainz, Germany, a major center of early printing.</note>
---
### 1633.
<note>The Roman numerals "M. DC. XXXIII." indicate the year 1633.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for a 1633 edition of the Enchiridion of Pope Leo, a legendary collection of prayers and charms supposedly gifted by Pope Leo III to Emperor Charlemagne.</summary>

<keywords>Enchiridion, Pope Leo III, Charlemagne, Mainz, 17th-century printing, Chi-Rho symbol, protective prayers</keywords>

| 24 | b | St. Alexander <note>original: "s. Alexandre"; the companion of St. Epipodius, martyred in Lyon shortly after his friend.</note> |

| 26 | d | St. Marcellinus <note>original: "s. Marcel"; an early Pope and martyr during the Diocletianic Persecution.</note> |
| 27 | e | St. Anastasius <note>original: "s. Anaſtaſe"; likely Pope Anastasius I, who was praised by St. Jerome for his holiness.</note> |
| 28 | f | St. Vitalis <note>original: "s. Vital"; a 1st or 2nd-century martyr of Milan, traditionally considered the father of Saints Gervasius and Protasius.</note> |
| 29 | g | St. Peter Martyr <note>original: "s. Pierre M."; a 13th-century Dominican friar and inquisitor who was assassinated; he is a major saint of the Dominican Order.</note> |
| 30 | A | St. Eutropius <note>original: "s. Eutrope"; the first bishop of Saintes in France and a celebrated martyr.</note> |

# M A Y.

->May has 31 days, and the lunar cycle has 30<- <note>This note helps the reader calculate the "lunar month," which was essential for determining the date of Easter and other "moveable feasts" that change every year.</note>

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | b | St. James & St. Philip <note>original: "S. Jacques S. Philippes"; the joint feast of two of Christ's original twelve apostles.</note> |
| 2 | c | St. Athanasius <note>original: "s. Athanafe"; the 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria and a key defender of the Trinity against Arianism.</note> |
| 3 | d | The Holy Cross <note>original: "ste. Croix"; specifically the feast of the "Invention" (Finding) of the Holy Cross by St. Helena.</note> |
| 4 | e | St. Helena <note>original: "ste. Heleine"; the mother of Emperor Constantine, credited with discovering the True Cross in Jerusalem.</note> |
| 5 | f | St. Fortunatus <note>original: "s. Fortunat"; likely St. Fortunatus of Amiens, a 4th-century martyr.</note> |
| 6 | g | **ST. JOHN AT THE LATIN GATE** <note>original: "s. JEAN PORTE LATINE"; this feast commemorates a tradition where St. John the Apostle was thrown into a vat of boiling oil near the Latin Gate in Rome but emerged unharmed.</note> |
| 7 | A | St. Stanislaus <note>original: "s. Stanislas"; the Bishop of Kraków and patron saint of Poland, martyred by King Bolesław II.</note> |
| 8 | b | Apparition of St. Michael <note>original: "Ap. s. Michel"; commemorates the appearance of the Archangel Michael on Mount Gargano in Italy in the 5th century.</note> |

| 10 | d | St. Gordian <note>original: "s. Gordian"; a Roman martyr who was beheaded during the reign of Julian the Apostate.</note> |

# HANDBOOK
<note>original: "Enchiridion"; from the Greek word for a manual or book meant to be kept "in the hand" for constant use.</note>

## SENT TO THE MOST SERENE
## Emperor of the French
## Charlemagne, by
<note>Charles the Great (r. 768–814 AD), who united much of Western and Central Europe.</note>
## Pope Leo, to make
<note>Pope Leo III, who crowned Charlemagne as Emperor in 800 AD.</note>
## him happy in all
## the events of life.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'L' with floral scrollwork background.</image-desc>

The history of past centuries bears witness that among all Sovereign Princes, there has been none until now who lived more happily than <term>Charlemagne</term> <note>Charles the Great, King of the Franks and first Holy Roman Emperor</note>: In times of peace and in times of war, his undertakings always enjoyed a favorable <insert>suc-</insert> <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "suc-" indicates the next page begins with "succès" (success).</note>

<summary>This title page introduces a spiritual or magical handbook purportedly gifted by Pope Leo III to Charlemagne to ensure his success and happiness. It establishes the legendary status of Charlemagne as a uniquely fortunate ruler.</summary>
<keywords>Enchiridion, Charlemagne, Pope Leo III, Frankish Emperor, success, manual of happiness, sovereignty</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues directly from the previous page's catchword "suc-," completing the word "success." It continues the historical and legendary justification for the handbook, attributing Charlemagne's triumphs to his spiritual devotion and his relationship with the Papacy.</meta>

success: his valor has shone out a hundred times in perilous circumstances where anyone other than he would have succumbed. He faced death with equal intrepidity in <term>pitched battles</term> <note>Formal battles where both armies are drawn up in prepared positions, as opposed to skirmishes.</note> as at the sieges of cities which he undertook to bring under his obedience.

A victor over his declared enemies as well as secret assassins, he successfully escaped all the dangers to which a Prince—whose glory and great merit provoke jealousy—is ordinarily exposed.

Do you wish to know, <term>friend reader</term> <note>A common rhetorical device in 17th and 18th-century literature to create a personal connection with the audience.</note>, what was the source of such great prosperity? He confessed it himself in a letter of thanks addressed to Pope Leo, the original of which may still be seen in Rome in the <term>Vatican Library</term> <note>The official library of the Holy See, one of the oldest and most significant collections of historical texts in the world.</note>, written in his own hand in a very simple style, but one which expresses quite <term>artlessly</term> <note>original: "naïvement"; meaning with sincerity, naturalness, and without guile.</note> the greatness of his gratitude toward...

<summary>This page describes Charlemagne's legendary military success and his miraculous survival of assassination attempts. It attributes his prosperity to a secret source revealed in a letter of thanks to Pope Leo III, allegedly held in the Vatican Library.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the letter of thanks from Charlemagne to Pope Leo III, specifically describing the contents and impact of the mystical gift he received.</meta>

...this <term>Sovereign Pontiff</term> <note>A title for the Pope, here referring to Leo III (reigned 795–816 AD).</note>. He indicates to him in this letter that since he received a small booklet entitled <term>Enchiridion</term> <note>From the Greek word meaning "handbook" or "manual." In this context, it refers to the legendary grimoire of prayers and charms attributed to the Pope.</note>, filled with particular prayers and several <term>mysterious figures</term> <note>Refers to the occult diagrams, seals, and talismans typically found in magical manuscripts.</note> sent by his Holiness as a precious gift, he has not ceased to be fortunate. He declares in this same letter that everything found in the Universe capable of harming man has had no malice against him, and that all the wonders which he had experienced within himself by the <term>virtue</term> <note>In this historical context, "virtue" refers to the inherent spiritual power or efficacy of an object or prayer.</note> of this small booklet, had led him to have it transcribed in <term>golden characters</term> <note>Writing in gold ink (chrysography) was a mark of extreme prestige, reserved for the most sacred texts or imperial decrees.</note> and to apply himself to reading it with the reverence that he had recommended to him: that furthermore, all his gratitude for such a rare gift being unable to equal his obligations, he could do no better than to devote himself and his Empire without reserve to the service of his Holiness, to whom he wish-

The curious reader will perhaps be very pleased to see a faithful copy of this Latin letter, taken word-for-word from its original in the <term>Vatican</term> <note>The headquarters of the Roman Catholic Church in Rome; the author claims this text is a direct copy of an official historical record.</note>, and whose contents I have just explained in English <note>original: "françois" (French). The author is referring to the summary of the letter provided on the preceding pages.</note> for the benefit of those who do not understand Latin, and whom it is appropriate to instruct by such authentic testimony of the excellence of this marvelous <term>little book</term> <note>Referring to the *Enchiridion*, a grimoire or handbook of prayers and mystical figures.</note>.

# LETTER
## From the Emperor Charlemagne to Pope Leo.

Charlemagne, the most fortunate Emperor of the Gauls and of Germany <note>original: "Gallorum & Germaniæ Imperator"</note>, by the grace of God and the effectiveness of the precious <term>little book</term> <note>original: "libelli"; referring to the Enchiridion, a handbook of mystical prayers and protections.</note> given to him as a humble gift by the most holy Lord Pope Leo, the Supreme Pontiff, returns his most profound thanks.

Highest of the high priests <note>original: "Summe Antistitum Antistites"</note>, I owe more to your Holiness for the little book which you sent to us eight months ago, than I owe to nature for the empire to which she raised me without any other assistance. For indeed, I have experienced wonders through this marvelous little book that entirely surpass the forces of nature. Truly, death flees; enemies are scattered; those who conspired against me are rendered motionless; poisons have no effect, nor do daggers or swords; in a single word, I shall say,

<summary>This page contains the opening of a letter from Charlemagne to Pope Leo III, in which the Emperor expresses his profound gratitude for the "little book" (the Enchiridion), claiming its divine power exceeds even his imperial authority.</summary>
<keywords>Charlemagne, Pope Leo III, gratitude, divine protection, Enchiridion, miracles, imperial testimony</keywords>

all things which in the course of nature are

little book <term>codex</term> <note>original: "parvuli codicis"; a codex is a bound book, here referring to the Enchiridion.</note>. For that

### *Instructions on what one should practice to make effective use of this little book.*

When one has been fortunate enough to possess a copy of this little book, it must be placed in a small bag of new leather <note>Storing a magical or holy book in "new leather" was a common practice intended to preserve its purity and protect it from "profane" contact.</note> so that it may be properly preserved. Afterward, one shall make a vow to carry it upon oneself as much as possible, and not to fail each day to read devoutly and with attention at least one page. If one foresees or fears some peril, one must read a page suitable to what is feared; this can be done easily by noting the titles of the prayers <term>prayers</term> <note>original: "oraisons"; these are formal, petitionary prayers, often used in a ritual context for protection or assistance.</note>. It must be observed that while reading this book, one should be on one's knees with the face turned toward the rising Sun <note>The practice of praying toward the East (the rising sun) is a tradition found in both early Christianity and various Western esoteric systems, representing a turn toward the source of light and life.</note>, as did

### Penitential.

And my soul is also greatly troubled; but you, O Lord—how long? <note>original: "Et anima mea turbata est valde; sed tu Domine usquequo?"</note>

Turn back, O Lord, and rescue my soul; save me for the sake of your mercy <note>original: "propter misericordiam tuam"</note>.

For in death there is no one who remembers you; and in the <term>underworld</term> <note>original: "inferno"; in this context, it refers to the realm of the dead (often called Sheol in Hebrew), where the soul was thought to be cut off from the living world and unable to offer liturgical praise.</note>, who will give you praise? <note>original: "quis confitebitur tibi?"</note>

I am worn out with my moaning; every night I will wash my bed: I will soak my couch with my tears.

My eye is wasted away because of my grief <note>original: "a furore"; while often meaning "fury," in the context of the Psalms it refers to the inner turmoil or vexation caused by suffering.</note>; I have grown old among all my enemies.

Depart from me, all you who work iniquity: for the Lord has heard the voice of my weeping.

The Lord has heard my supplication: the Lord has received my prayer.

Let all my enemies be ashamed and greatly troubled; let them be turned back and put to shame very quickly.

Glory be to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Gloria Patri, &c."; the speaker begins the Minor Doxology, a standard hymn of praise used to conclude Psalms in Christian liturgy.</note>.

## Psalm 31. <note>In modern Bibles that follow the Hebrew numbering, this is known as Psalm 32. It is a "Maskil" or a song of instruction concerning the joy of forgiveness.</note>

Blessed are those whose iniquities are forgiven: and whose sins are covered. <note>original: "Beati quorum remissæ sunt iniquitates : & quorum tecta sunt peccata."</note>

Blessed is the man to whom the Lord has not <term>imputed</term> <note>original: "imputavit"; in a legal or theological sense, to "impute" means to reckon or attribute a fault to someone's account. Because I kept silent, my bones grew old: while I cried out all day long. <note>original: "Quoniam tacui, inveteraverunt ossa mea"; the speaker describes how the internal pressure of unconfessed sin led to physical wasting and exhaustion.</note>

I said, I will confess my injustice against myself to the Lord: and you forgave the <term>impiety</term> <note>original: "impietatem"; referring to the lack of reverence or the "unholiness" of the act.</note> of my sin.

For this every holy one shall pray to you: in a seasonable time.

...me, they drew near and stood. <note>original: "verſum me appropinquaverunt & ſteterunt." This completes the sentence from the previous page where the speaker's inner circle abandons him during his illness.</note>

But I, like a deaf man, did not hear: and I was like a mute person who does not open his mouth. <note>original: "ſicut mutus non aperiens os ſuum." This highlights the "patient suffering" trope, where the penitent accepts his affliction without defensive argument.</note>

And I became like a man who does not hear: and who has no <term>rebukes</term> <note>original: "redargutiones"; this refers to a counter-argument or a defensive reply in a legal or moral sense. The speaker offers no defense for his actions.</note> in his mouth.

Because in you, O Lord, have I hoped: you will hear me, O Lord my God.

For I said: Let my enemies never rejoice over me: and while my feet are moved, they spoke great things against me. <note>The "moving of feet" is a biblical metaphor for slipping, failing, or losing one's social or moral standing.</note>

But my enemies live, and they have grown stronger than I: and those who hate me wrongfully are multiplied. <note>original: "multiplicati ſunt qui oderunt me iniquè." The speaker feels overwhelmed by the sheer number of those opposing him without cause.</note>

### Psalm 50 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 51. Known by its first word in Latin as the *Miserere*, it is the most famous of the seven Penitential Psalms, traditionally attributed to King David expressing his repentance.</note>

Have mercy on me, O God: according to your great mercy.

And according to the multitude of your tender mercies: <term>blot out</term> <note>original: "dele"; meaning to erase or wipe away, as one would erase writing from a tablet, signifying the total removal of the record of sin.</note> my iniquity.

Wash me yet more from my iniquity: and cleanse me from my sin.

For I know my iniquity: and my sin is always before me. <note>The speaker acknowledges that his guilt is not a fleeting thought but a constant, haunting presence in his conscience.</note>

To you alone have I sinned, and done evil before...

[I have done evil before] you: so that you may be justified in your words, and prevail when you are judged. <note>original: "ut justificeris in sermonibus tuis, & vincas cum judicaris." This verse acknowledges that God is the ultimate moral authority and is always right in His judgments.</note>

# Penitential Psalms
<note>original: "Penitentiaux." This is a collection of seven psalms traditionally used in the Church to express sorrow for sin.</note>

...incline your ear to me. <note>The speaker asks God to listen closely, as one might lean in to hear a desperate whisper.</note>
In whatever day I shall call upon you:
speedily hear me.
For my days have vanished like smoke: and my bones have dried up like brushwood. <note>original: "cremium." This refers to dry firewood or kindling that burns away rapidly, a metaphor for a life consumed by suffering.</note>
I am smitten like grass, and my heart is withered: because I have forgotten to eat my bread. <note>The speaker is so overwhelmed by sorrow that they have lost the basic desire to sustain themselves with food.</note>
From the voice of my groaning: my bone has cleaved to my flesh. <note>A vivid description of becoming "skin and bone" due to grief or fasting.</note>
I have become like a pelican of the wilderness: I have become like a night-owl in the ruins. <note>original: "nicticorax in domicilio." The pelican and the owl were common symbols of solitude and mourning, representing a soul that feels abandoned and isolated.</note>
I have kept watch, and have become like a sparrow sitting alone upon the housetop.
All the day long my enemies reproached me: and they who once praised me now swear against me.
For I ate ashes like bread: and mixed my drink with weeping. <note>In ancient traditions, sitting in ashes and "eating" them signified the deepest possible state of penance and mourning.</note>
Because of your wrath and indignation: for you have lifted me up and cast me down.
My days have declined like a shadow: and I have withered like grass. <note>The "declining shadow" refers to the lengthening shadows of evening that signal the end of the day, or in this case, the end of a life.</note>

## Psalm 129. <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 130. It is one of the most famous of the "Penitential Psalms," used throughout history in funeral rites and prayers for the deceased.</note>

Out of the depths I have cried to you, O Lord: Lord, hear my voice. <note>original: "De profundis clamavi." This phrase has become a universal expression for a cry of deep despair or intense prayer from a place of suffering.</note>

Let your ears be attentive: to the voice of my supplication.

If you, Lord, should mark iniquities: Lord, who could stand? <note>This verse acknowledges human imperfection, suggesting that if God kept a strict record of every sin, no one would be found worthy.</note>

For with you there is forgiveness: and because of your law, I have waited for you, O Lord. <note>original: "propitiatio." This refers to God’s mercy and his willingness to be reconciled with humanity.</note>

My soul has relied on his word: my soul has hoped in the Lord.

From the morning watch even until night: let Israel hope in the Lord.

For with the Lord there is mercy: and with him is plenteous redemption. <note>original: "copiosa... redemptio." The author emphasizes that God's ability to save is not just sufficient, but "abundant" or "overflowing."</note>

And he shall redeem Israel: from all his iniquities.

Glory be to the Father, etc. <note>original: "Gloria Patri." This is the Lesser Doxology, a standard Christian conclusion to the recitation of any Psalm, affirming the Trinity.</note>

## Psalm 142. <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 143. It is the last of the seven Penitential Psalms, traditionally attributed to King David during a time of great personal distress.</note>

Hear my prayer, O Lord; give ear to my supplication in your truth: hear me in your justice.

# Litanies

> That You would deign to humble the enemies of Your holy Church, we beseech You. <note>original: "Ut inimicos sanctæ Ecclesiæ humiliare digneris." In a historical context, this petition sought protection against those who physically or spiritually threatened the institution of the Church.</note>
> That You would deign to grant peace and true concord to Christian Kings and Princes, we beseech You. <term>Concord: from the Latin "concordia," referring to a state of agreement or harmony, particularly important for maintaining political stability in Christendom.</term>
> That You would deign to bestow peace and unity upon all Christian people, we beseech You.
> That You would deign to strengthen and preserve us in Your holy service, we beseech You. <note>original: "in tuo sancto servitio." This refers to the dedicated life of prayer and labor performed by the clergy or the faithful.</note>
> That You would lift our minds to heavenly desires, we beseech You, hear us. <note>original: "ad cœlestia desideria." A central theme in Christian mysticism, asking for the focus of the heart to shift from temporary earthly concerns to eternal spiritual ones.</note>
> That You would repay all our benefactors with everlasting blessings, we beseech You, hear us. <term>Benefactors: individuals who provided financial or material support to the church, monastery, or community.</term>
> That You would rescue our souls, and those of our brothers, relatives, and benefactors, from eternal damnation, we beseech You, hear us.
> That You would deign to give and preserve the fruits of the earth, we beseech You, hear us. <note>original: "fructus terræ." A crucial prayer for agricultural communities, asking God to provide a good harvest and protect it from blight or storm.</note>
> That to all the faithful departed <note>original: "omnibus fidelibus defunctis." This petition typically concludes with a request for eternal rest, but the sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>This page contains a series of communal petitions from the Litany of the Saints, asking for the humility of enemies, political peace, spiritual perseverance, and a successful harvest. It highlights the integration of spiritual needs, such as rescue from damnation, with practical medieval concerns like agricultural survival.</summary>

->**The Perils of the World**<-

How great is the blindness of men, who seek their happiness in the midst of the storms of this world! They build upon sand, and they wonder when the edifice of their fortune crumbles at the first breath of wind. <note>A reference to the biblical parable in Matthew 7:24-27 regarding the wise and foolish builders.</note>

It is a constant truth that *the world passes away, and its desires with it* <note>original: "mundus transit et concupiscentia ejus" from 1 John 2:17</note>; yet we cling to it as if it were an eternal dwelling. We are like travelers who, finding a pleasant meadow, forget the destination of their journey and lie down to sleep, unmindful of the night that approaches.

Consider, O soul, the words of the Prophet: *God shall destroy thee forever, he shall take thee away, and pluck thee out of thy dwelling place, and root thee out of the land of the living.* <note>original: "Deus destruet te in finem..." from Psalm 52:5</note> What then will remain of your pomps, your riches, and those vain honors that you pursued with such ardor?

<column-break/>

The <term>tabernacle</term> <note>A temporary dwelling or tent, often used in spiritual texts to symbolize the human body or earthly life.</note> of your body shall be folded up, and your spirit shall stand naked before the Sovereign Judge. There, the <insert>false</insert> lights of the world will no longer deceive you. You will see things as they truly are: the gold as dross, the pleasures as bitterness, and the honors as smoke.

Why then do you delay? *Turn to the Lord your God* <note>original: "Convertere ad Dominum Deum tuum"</note> while there is still time. Do not wait for the final hour to detach your heart from these fleeting shadows. The <term>apple orchard</term> <note>Likely a metaphor for the "fruits" of worldly labor or the Garden of Eden's temptation.</note> of this life may seem sweet to the taste, but its fruit is often filled with the ashes of regret.

Put your **Hope** in Him who does not change. For God is the only rock that remains standing when the world and all its vanities have vanished away.

<summary>This page warns the reader about the fleeting nature of worldly success and the inevitability of divine judgment, urging a focus on eternal spiritual truths over earthly vanity.</summary>
<keywords>vanity, judgment, spiritual blindness, impermanence, repentance, Psalm 52</keywords>

...one of my disciples, named Thaddeus, so that he may cure you of every infirmity and weakness: and he shall grant life to those who are with you. These are the words that Adam spoke when he was in Hell, or in Limbo <note>In medieval theology, Limbo was the place where the souls of the righteous who died before Christ awaited redemption</note>, upon the banks of the Acheron <note>A river in the underworld of Greek mythology, often conflated with Hell in later occult texts</note>. If anyone carries these words upon themselves in war, they shall not be killed nor wounded by anyone whomsoever. Likewise shall it be for the traveler who carries them for the space of 70 days; he shall never be captured on the road nor attacked by thieves, and he shall have time to find a Priest to be heard in confession and receive remission for his sins. It is also of great virtue for those who travel by sea; it delivers from peril anyone who carries it with great devotion.

# The Perils of the World

<margin>The misfortunes of the goods of marriage and a false charm which prevents</margin> Those things by which marriage is prevented from producing its effects are not among the least of troubles: for along with the distress they bring to households and families, they sometimes cause simple people of little faith to profane the said holy Sacrament. These people allow themselves to be persuaded that they must renounce the first Sacrament and celebrate it anew to be delivered from their <term>maléfice</term> <note>A "maléfice" refers to a curse or act of sorcery, specifically "ligature" or "tying the knot," believed to cause impotence in newlyweds.</note>. This is a purely diabolical invention and a thing that must by no means be done; for it is the express word of God in Saint Matthew, chapter 19:

->What God has joined together, let no man put asunder.<-
<note>original Latin: "Quod Deus conjunxit, homô non separet."</note>

That is why I have thought it expedient—both for the consolation of afflicted persons and to thwart the design of Satan in this matter—to set down here this remedy.

The first thing that must...

<summary>This page warns against the "diabolical" practice of re-marrying to break a sorcerous curse, citing the Gospel of Matthew to uphold the permanence of the marriage sacrament. The author intends to provide a spiritual remedy for those afflicted by such charms.</summary>
<keywords>marriage, sacraments, sorcery, witchcraft, spiritual remedies, Gospel of Matthew, Satan, domestic life</keywords>

# Prayers against <note>The header "Oraisons contre" is incomplete here, likely referring to "Prayers against sorcery or enchantments."</note>

The first thing to be done by those who are <term>bewitched</term> <note>original: "maleficiez"; referring to those believed to be under a magical spell or curse</note> and hindered by any evil whatsoever, whether of the body or the mind, from enjoying the fruits of their marriage, is to diligently examine their conscience.

Secondly, they must confess as exactly as possible all of their sins, and ask for absolution.

Thirdly, they must hear a Mass of the Holy Spirit, and it would not be out of place for them to also receive Holy Communion, even though they are not obliged to do so by express commandment. Once the Mass has been celebrated, the Priest shall say for them the prayers that follow.

> V. Our help is in the name of the Lord. <note>original Latin: "Adjutorium nostrum in nomine Domini"</note>
> R. Who made heaven and earth. <note>original Latin: "Qui fecit cœlum & terram"</note>
> V. Save your servant and your handmaid. <note>original Latin: "Salvum fac servum tuum & ancillam tuam"</note>
> R. My God, who hope in you. <note>original Latin: "Deus meus sperantes in te"</note>

<header>Devout Prayer.</header>

### Prayer.

O Since you, heavenly Father, both in heaven and on earth, are that one almighty God in every nation, and shall remain so forever, I pray to you most humbly from my soul: do not allow us to turn this day toward my own will, nor let other believers turn it toward theirs; do not let us slip away into our own wicked judgment. Instead, let us rightly behold your fatherly will, your eternal counsel, that life-bringing word of yours, and your good pleasure. Grant that we may fulfill these things with a truthful heart, so that your divine name may be sanctified through us miserable sinners, now and forever. Through Christ our Lord. Amen. <note>original: "Per Christum Dominum nostrum. Amen." This is the standard liturgical conclusion to Latin prayers.</note>

<summary>A humble petition to the Heavenly Father asking for the grace to align one's personal will with the divine will throughout the day. It emphasizes the universal sovereignty of God and the desire for the sanctification of His name through the lives of believers.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, divine will, providence, sanctification, petition, daily devotion</keywords>

<margin>150</margin>
### *Devout Prayer.*
<note>original: "Dévote Orvison"</note>
...may He save us. Through Christ our Lord. Amen.

Saint Augustine says that regarding the prayer which follows, it is certain that on the day a person recites it, the devil shall not be able to harm them, nor shall they be troubled in any way; and whatever just thing they shall ask of our Lord, it shall be given to them. And when their soul departs from their body, it shall not be damned, provided that they have done their duty in serving God during their life.

### *Prayer.*
> O God, be merciful to me, a sinner <note>original: "Deus propitius efto mihi peccatori"</note>, and guard me; be with me all the days and nights of my life. O God of Abraham, God of Isaac, God of Jacob, have mercy on me, and send to my aid Saint Michael the Archangel...

## Devout Prayer.
> <margin>The Redeemer grants pardons; the following follows</margin>
> Hail, wound of the side of Christ the Redeemer, from which flowed the fountain of the stream; let it also be a medicine for the wretched against error. Now heal the wound of sorrow, and at the same time the wound of sin and error. <note>original: "Ave vulnus lateris Christi Redemptoris"</note> Hail, wide and fertile wound of the side: wash away the multitude of filths and cleanse me, lest the second death <note>A theological term referring to eternal damnation after physical death.</note> harm me in the lower regions, but let the mind become joyful by the sight of the Divine Power. Hail, sacred wound, among all fountains there is none like this sweet refreshment. Amen.

Pope Innocent IV <note>Reigned 1243–1254; he was known for formalizing many such spiritual grants.</note> has given three hundred days of pardons <term>pardons: also known as indulgences, these were remissions of temporal punishment for sins</term> for saying the prayer which follows.

> Bless me, imperial majesty; protect me, royal divinity; guard me, everlasting Deity; cherish me, glorious Unity; defend me, immense Trinity; direct me, inestimable goodness; protect me, the power of the Father; let the wisdom of the Son quicken me—

### Prayer to Cure Colic

### To See a Spirit

Fern or Brake-fern, <note>original: "Fougiere ou Feuchiere." This refers to the widespread European legend that the fern plant—which appears to have no seeds—blooms or drops invisible seeds only at midnight on Midsummer's Eve, granting the power to see spirits or become invisible.</note> I pray to God that the Spirits I desire may appear at exactly midnight, by saying these five words nine times: *Bar Kirha har alli alla.* <note>These are "words of power," likely derived from corrupted Hebrew or Aramaic, intended to sound ancient and authoritative.</note> One must do and say this at half-past eleven on the Eve of St. John <note>Midsummer's Eve, June 23rd, a night traditionally associated with high magic and the thinning of the veil between worlds.</note>.

## To stop bleeding.

Write with that same blood upon your hand, using the head of a pin: *Bacris*; and if it is a woman, *Borcas*. <note>These are "words of power" common in early modern grimoires; their specific etymology is often obscured, but they function as a verbal command to the body.</note>

## For a Nosebleed.

Write upon the belly or upon the forehead, using a straw dipped in the blood: *It is finished.* <note>original Latin: "Consummatum est." These were the final words of Jesus on the Cross (John 19:30). In historical folk magic, the use of Christ’s final words was believed to have the power to "finish" or stop a biological process, such as a hemorrhage or labor pains.</note>

## Against Fever.

Swallow a slip of paper <note>original: "un Billet." This refers to the practice of "ingestible magic," where a sacred word or prayer was written down and then eaten by the patient to internalize the spiritual cure.</note> upon which the following is written, for three consecutive days: ✠ Gla ✠ Garnaze ✠ Eglatus ✠ Egla. ✠ <note>"Egla" is a common variation of the well-known magical word "AGLA," an acronym for the Hebrew phrase "Atah Gibor Le-olam Adonai," meaning "Thou art mighty forever, O Lord."</note>

## *Prayer to heal an Ulcer.*

*First, prepare the compress, <note>original: "la compresse." In this period, this would be a piece of linen or cloth used to cover a wound.</note> of which you will make two pieces that you shall place in the shape of a cross,*

### Prayer against Hemorrhoids.

You must push them back three times with the middle finger of the right hand, saying each time the words that follow.

> **B**roka Broket <note>These appear to be rhyming "power words" or phonetic charms common in folk magic, likely intended to create a rhythmic resonance rather than a literal meaning.</note>, as God has made me; I have them no more by the grace of Jesus, in the name of the Father, & of the Son, & of the Holy Spirit. So be it.

### For Toothaches:

Write these words and wear them hanging around your neck: *Scrapers and hooks of the teeth, heal the pain of the teeth.* <note>original: "Stragiles falcesque dentale dentium dolorem persanate." This Latin charm uses rhythmic, tool-related imagery to command the pain to be "scraped" away or "cut."</note>

### To stop a Wolf, Deer, or Wild Boar.

Place a Topaz <note>A gemstone traditionally associated with cooling tempers and providing protection.</note> in your mouth along with a leaf of Southernwood <note>original: "Auronne" (*Artemisia abrotanum*). An aromatic herb frequently used in folk magic to ward off charms or wild animals.</note>; and upon seeing the animal, place them both in a small piece of taffeta, tie it up quickly, and throw it after the beast, saying three times in a loud voice: *Grabazon Ulion Adonay.* <note>These are "words of power." Adonay is a Hebrew name for God, often invoked in medieval and Renaissance magic to command the natural world.</note>

### To untie the Point.

<term>Untying the point: "L'aiguillette" (the lace) refers to a common folk-magic curse where a sorcerer would tie a knot in a cord during a wedding ceremony to magically render the groom impotent. "Untying" it was the ritual process of breaking this spell.</term>

Have the man urinate through the wedding ring on Friday morning at sunrise, saying this word: *Yemon.*

For anyone whose urine is stopped by a "pinning" <note>original: "cheviller." > From his side issued blood and water. <note>original: "De latere ejus exivit sanguis & aqua." This is a Latin quotation from the Gospel of John (19:34), referring to the wound in Christ’s side. ### For Quartan Fevers.

<term>Quartan Fever: A form of malaria characterized by paroxysms that occur every fourth day (every 72 hours). It was historically attributed to "black bile" or supernatural influences.</term>

### *Another for the same subject of forty days.*
<note>The "forty days" likely refers to the quartan fever—a form of malaria characterized by a paroxysm every fourth day—which was often treated with rituals lasting forty days or involving "forty" as a symbolic number.</note>

Take a small pin, such as a minikin pin <note>original: "camion," an archaic term for a very small, fine pin used in lace-making</note> with which one marks the left arm without leaving an impression <note>meaning to trace the words lightly on the skin without piercing or drawing blood</note>, three times as follows: "In the Name of the Father, & of the Son, & of the Holy Spirit," and attach the pin to the skin ✙ Glory ✙ Peace ✙. <note>original: "gloriâ pace." The crosses ✙ indicate where the sign of the cross should be made or where the symbol should be drawn on the skin.</note>

### *Against caterpillars on trees:*

*One must write the following on a long strip of paper; surround the tree with this paper, and let there be only a single line; all the caterpillars will fall, and there will be no more for the year.*

Christ reigns, Christ conquers, Christ commands you; there they have fallen, those who work iniquity, nor were they able to stand against the Lord. <note>original: "Christus regnat, Christus vincit, Christus vobis imperat, ibi ceciderunt qui operantur iniquitatem, nec potuerunt stare adversus Dominum." The first part of this text is a famous triumphal chant (the Laudes Regiae), while the second part is adapted from Psalm 36:12.</note>

<header>Secrets</header>

## *Prayer of Pope Leo, to remove all Spells & Enchantments, you shall say the following.*
<note>The "Prayer of Pope Leo" refers to a tradition stemming from the *Enchiridion of Pope Leo*, a famous grimoire of the Middle Ages and Renaissance. Legend claimed Pope Leo III gave a collection of protective charms to Charlemagne. These prayers were widely used as "breveries" or protective amulets.</note>

> LAsgaroth, ✛ Aphonidos, ✛ Palatin, ✛ Urat, ✛ Condion. ✛ Lamacron, ✛ Fondon, ✛ Arpagon, ✛ Alamar, ✛ Bourgafis let Serebani come. <note>original: "veniat Serebani." The names used here are *voces magicae*—mystical words of power common in ritual magic. The crosses ✛ indicate where the speaker should make the sign of the cross.</note>

### *To discover Thieves.*
<note>original: "Pour découvrir les Larrens." A "larron" is an archaic French term for a thief or brigand.</note>

You shall write separately on paper all the names of those who are in the house, masters, servants & others; throw the slips of paper into a bronze pot <note>original: "poële d’airain." Bronze or brass was often specified in ritual magic because of its historical and biblical associations.</note> full of clear water, then say over it: I conjure you ONAZARDE, AROGANI, LABILAFO, PARANDO, AXIGOULA, MARACTATON, SIRANDAY, ENTALEFON, LAMBOURED; then, if the name of the Thief is in the pot, it will rise to the surface of the water.

<summary>This page contains a protective prayer attributed to Pope Leo III and a divination ritual using a bronze basin of water to identify a thief among a household's residents.</summary>

### Way of winning over one's own genius & finding it.

The Most High having given to every man at the moment of his birth two spirits <note>original: "génies." During the Renaissance, "Christian Cabala" became popular among scholars who sought to use divine names and mystical symbols to communicate with the spiritual world.</note> have found the means to win it over; if therefore one wishes to know one's good genius, and make it affable and familiar so as to learn secrets from it, and have the knowledge of all the sciences, you shall say three times the Prayer <note>original: "Oraison."</note> which follows.

> Almighty eternal God, who created all of creation for your praise and your honor, and for the service of man, I pray by your ineffable name, for my genius. <note>original: "Omnipotens æterne Deus , qui totam creaturam condidisti in laudem tuam & honorem tuum , & ministerium hominis , oro per ineffabile nomen tuum , ut genium meum."</note> Amen.

## *Prayer to protect oneself from firearms, say:*

Do not betray our Lord Jesus Christ. Amen. <note>original Latin: "Non tradas Dominum noſtrum Jeſum Chriſtum." This prayer draws a symbolic parallel between the firing of a weapon and the betrayal of Christ, effectively "binding" the weapon so it cannot perform its function.</note>

->Ales † Dales † Tales †.<- <note>These are "magical names" or "words of power" common in grimoires of this era, intended to create a spiritual shield around the wearer.</note>

## For Eye Ailments.

Saint John passing by here, encountered three Virgins on his path; he said to them, "What are you doing here?" "We are healing the cataract <note>original: *maille*, literally 'mesh' or 'link,' referring to a white spot, film, or cataract on the eye.</note>." "O heal, Virgins, heal the eye of N. <note>The letter 'N.' serves as a placeholder where the practitioner would speak the name of the afflicted person.</note>." Making the sign of the Cross and blowing into the eye, he continues: "Cataract, grievous fire <note>original: *feu grief*, likely referring to shingles, erysipelas, or intense ocular inflammation.</note>, whatever fire it may be, growths <note>original: *ongles*, literally 'nails,' a historical term for a pterygium or a fleshy growth on the conjunctiva.</note>, migraine, and spider-spots <note>original: *arraignée*, likely referring to 'muscae volitantes' or spots in the vision that look like webs.</note>, I command you to have no more power over this eye than the Jews had on Easter day over the Body of Our Lord Jesus Christ." Then one makes the sign of the Cross again and blows into the eye of the sick person, ordering them to say three Our Fathers <note>original: *Pater*</note> and three Hail Marys <note>original: *Ave*</note>, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit.

# TABLE OF CONTENTS.

## BLESSING.

->God of Abraham, God of Isaac, God of Jacob.<-

Bless, O Lord, this <term>creature</term><note>In ritual practice, "creature" (from the Latin *creatura*) refers to any physical substance or object—such as a talisman, salt, or water—that is being consecrated for spiritual use.</note> [N.] <note>The "N." serves as a placeholder for the name of the specific object being blessed.</note> so that, being blessed and sanctified in honor of your holy Name, it may drive away from all those who carry or look upon it all phantoms <note>Deceptive or harmful spirits.</note> or harmful enemies; this is what we ask of you through your only Son, Our Lord Jesus Christ <note>original: "N. S. J. C." (Notre Seigneur Jésus Christ).</note>, who lives and reigns, etc.

Enchiridion <note>Literally a "handbook" or "manual." The *Enchiridion of Pope Leo III* is a well-known grimoire containing prayers and protective charms, historically (though likely apocryphally) said to have been gifted by the Pope to Charlemagne for his protection.</note> sent to the Most Serene Emperor of the French <note>The text uses "François" (French) here, though historically Charlemagne was King of the Franks.</note>, Charlemagne, by Pope Leo. page 1

Letter from the Emperor Charlemagne to Pope Leo. 5

Instructions on what one must practice to usefully employ [this book]...

<vocab>Blessing, God of Abraham, Enchiridion, Charlemagne, Pope Leo</vocab>

The Beginning of the Holy Gospel according to John. <note>original: "Initium sancti Evangelii secundum Joannem." This passage (John 1:1–14) was historically recited or carried as an amulet for protection against evil.</note> 13

travel. 78

*Admirable Prayer to the Cross of the Savior J. C. 79* <note>original: "J. C." refers to Jesus Christ.</note>

Prayer to the Virgin Mary. 82

Very useful prayers when using the bones of a dead man. 84 <note>This suggests a form of necromantic or folk-magic practice where physical relics are used to amplify the efficacy of the prayer.</note>

Prayer to make a woman faithful. 89

Mysterious Pentacles. 95 <note>original: "Pantacules." In grimoires, these are complex geometric talismans or sigils believed to hold spiritual power or command over spirits.</note>

Prayer of Saint Leo, written to Charles, King of France, to preserve him from an evil death, and to be "shod on ice." 103 <note>original: "ferré à glace." While literally referring to horseshoes designed for ice, here it is an idiom meaning to be perfectly prepared, secure, and invulnerable against all dangers.</note>

Prayer to conjure all weapons.

## T A B L E.

... armed. 107

115

Prayer to the Savior J. C. for the same subject. 117 <note>original: "J. C." is an abbreviation for Jesus Christ (Jésus-Christ).</note>

The words that Pope Leo sent to Charlemagne, to preserve him from fire and water; if a pregnant woman carries them on her person, she will be relieved. 118 <note>This refers to the *Enchiridion of Pope Leo*, a famous grimoire legend claiming that Pope Leo III gave a collection of protective charms to Emperor Charlemagne to protect him from all earthly dangers.</note>

These are the Names of J. C.; whoever reads them, or has them read, will be loved by all. 120 <note>The "Names of Jesus" or "Names of God" were often used as a list-based charm to gain favor or social charisma.</note>

The words spoken by Adam, to preserve against all kinds of wounds. 121 <note>In traditional folk magic, Adam was often credited with knowing the primordial names of things, which granted power over physical harm.</note>

The holy Names sent to King Fabritius, so as not to be captured or killed, tested on animals. 123 <note>Gaius Fabricius Luscinus was a Roman consul known for his integrity; however, in this context, he is a legendary figure receiving a magical protection charm. The note "tested on animals" was a common marketing device in grimoires to demonstrate a spell's practical efficacy.</note>

Words of J. C. to preserve the one who carries them on a journey by sea or on the river; he will be free from danger. 125

Prayer to Saint Michael, for those who travel on the water. 126 <note>Saint Michael the Archangel was frequently invoked as a general protector against the perils of nature and spiritual enemies.</note>

Prayer to be said in the evening before going to bed. 127

# TABLE.

*Our Savior received [certain] Wounds during his Passion. 153* <note>original: "Playes." Devotions in the late medieval and early modern periods often focused on the specific number of wounds Christ suffered—traditionally numbering in the thousands—as a source of protective power.</note>
*Other Prayers of Pope Leo, of great virtue. 154* <note>original: "Pape Leon." Likely referring to the *Enchiridion of Pope Leo*, a famous collection of protective charms and prayers traditionally attributed to Pope Leo III and said to have been gifted to Charlemagne.</note>

*Prayer of Pope John XX for the same subject. 157* <note>There was no actual Pope John XX due to a historical numbering error; in grimoires, this name often appears for prayers attributed to the medieval papacy.</note>
*Prayer that Pope Innocent gave in honor of the Wound of the Savior J. C. 158* <note>original: "J. C." abbreviated for Jesus Christ.</note>

*Admirable virtues of a Figure. 164* <note>In this context, a "Figure" refers to a talismanic drawing, sigil, or geometric diagram used for magical protection or influence.</note>

->the same.<- <note>original: "Avives." A historical term for an inflammation or swelling of the parotid glands in horses, often called "vives" or "strangles" in old English veterinary texts.</note>

# TABLE.

Other Prayers to the Cross of the Savior. 128

Prayer of great virtue, which Pope Leo sent to Charlemagne, to preserve him from the waters. [on] the same [page]. <note>original: "Leon Pape." This refers to the *Enchiridion of Pope Leo*, a popular collection of protective prayers and charms that legend claims Pope Leo III gifted to Emperor Charlemagne to shield him from harm.</note>

Prayer of the venerable Bede, against all sorts of shipwrecks & perils. 130 <note>Bede (673–735 AD) was a famous English monk and scholar; his name was often invoked in medieval and early modern spiritual protections for travelers.</note>

Devout Prayer that the people must say to God, to appease the contagion. 132 <note>original: "la contagion." This refers to the plague or other widespread infectious diseases, which were often met with communal prayer as a form of spiritual medicine.</note>

Very devout Prayer to the true Cross of J. C. 135 <note>original: "J. C." abbreviated for Jesus Christ.</note>

Singular remedy to preserve oneself from the spells of <term>knot-tiers</term>. 136 <note>original: "noueurs d'éguillettes." This refers to a specific type of folk magic known as "ligature." It was believed that a sorcerer could tie knots in a cord or leather lace (an "aiguillette") during a wedding ceremony to cause male impotence or prevent the marriage from being consummated.</note>

Prayer to say in the morning to protect oneself against one’s enemies. 144

Prayer of St. Augustine which protects from the evil Spirit during the day, & warns of the hour of death. 150 <note>original: "malin Esprit." Traditional belief held that certain prayers of St. Augustine could provide both daily protection from demons and a supernatural premonition of when one was about to die.</note>

Prayer of Pope Benedict II, which grants as much Indulgence as... <note>original: "Indulgence." In Catholic doctrine, an indulgence is the remission of temporal punishment for sins. Popular prayer books often claimed that reciting certain prayers could earn the believer thousands of years of indulgence.</note>

[A circular woodcut emblem is centered on the page. In the middle is a modified **Chi-Rho** <note>A "Christogram" formed by overlaying the first two letters of the Greek word for Christ: Chi (X) and Rho (P).</note> monogram. The Rho is modified with a horizontal crossbar to resemble a cross, and the base of the vertical stem rests upon a crescent shape. This central symbol is encircled by a double-ruled border containing a Latin motto.]

->CONQUER BY THIS<- <note>original: "VINCE HOC." This is a variant of the famous Latin phrase *In hoc signo vinces* ("In this sign you shall conquer"), which tradition says the Roman Emperor Constantine saw in a vision before his victory at the Battle of the Milvian Bridge.</note>
